K15.609-610K. XV 609–610, ch. 2.47, 2.48 difficulty low (0.68/100; thin 1, absent 0) (a) fail (b) pass 4/5 (c) pass 10/10 unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἅπαντα οὖν τὰ τοιαῦτα δοκεῖ μοι δηλῶσαι διὰ τῆσδε τῆς λέξεως ὁ Ἱπποκράτης, ἐν ᾗ φησιν· ὅσα τε ἡμέων 610 ἡ φύσις καὶ ἕξις ἑκάστοισιν ἐκτεκνοῖ πάθεα καὶ εἴδεα παντοῖα· καὶ νῦν φύσιν λέγει τὴν κρᾶσιν, ἕξιν δὲ αὐτοῦ τοῦ σώματος τὴν κατασκευήν. οἱ μὲν γὰρ ἀραιότεροι φύσει διαφοροῦνται καὶ συγκόπτονται |ῥᾳδίως, οἱ δὲ πυκνότεροι χρονίζουσιν ἐν ταῖς νόσοις. οὕτω δὲ καὶ μόρια τὰ μὲν ἔχουσί τινες ψυχρότερα, τὰ δὲ θερμότερα, καὶ τὰ μὲν ξηρότερα, τὰ δὲ ὑγρότερα· ἅπασι δὲ οἰκεῖον τῇ κράσει τὸ νόσημα. Μεῖζον μὲν γὰρ κακόν ἐστιν, ἢν διὰ τὸν πόνον καὶ τὴν ὀξύτητα τῆς νούσου ἀσθενέοντι προσφέρῃ τις ποτὸν ἢ ῥόφημα πλεῖον ἢ σιτίον, οἰόμενος διὰ κενεαγγείην ἀσθενέειν. ἀεικὲς δὲ καὶ διὰ κενεαγγείην ἀσθενέοντα μὴ γνῶναι καὶ πιέζειν τῇ διαίτῃ. φέρει μὲν γάρ τινα κίνδυνον καὶ αὕτη ἡ ἁμαρτὰς, πολλῷ δὲ ἧσσον τῆς ἑτέρης· καταγελαστοτέρη δὲ πολλῷ αὕτη μᾶλλον ἡ ἁμαρτὰς τῆς ἑτέρης.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

All such things, then, Hippocrates seems to me to have indicated through this phrase, in which he says: 'and all the affections and forms of every kind that our 610 nature and habit beget in each of us'; and here he calls the mixture 'nature', and the constitution of the body itself 'habit'. For those who are by nature more rarefied are easily dispersed and fall into syncope, whereas the denser ones linger long in their diseases. So too some people have certain parts colder, others hotter, and some drier, others moister; and in all cases the disease is akin to the mixture. For it is a greater evil if, on account of the pain and the acuteness of the disease, one administers to a person who is weak more drink or gruel or food, supposing that he is weak on account of emptiness of the vessels. But it is also unseemly not to recognize one who is weak on account of emptiness of the vessels and to press him hard with the regimen. For this error too carries some danger, but much less than the other; yet this error is far more ridiculous than the other.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: διαφοροῦνται

All such things, then, Hippocrates seems to me to have indicated through this phrase, in which he says: 'and all the affections and forms of every kind that our nature and condition engender in each of us' 610; and here he calls 'nature' the mixture (krasis), and 'condition' (hexis) the constitution of the body itself. For those who are by nature of looser texture are dissipated and fall into syncope easily, while those of denser texture are protracted in their diseases. So too some people have certain parts colder and others hotter, some drier and others moister; and in all of them the disease is proper to the mixture. For it is a greater evil if, to a patient who is weak because of the pain and the acuteness of the disease, one administers more drink or gruel or food, supposing that he is weak because of emptiness of the vessels. But it is also shameful not to recognize a patient who is weak because of emptiness of the vessels, and to press him hard with the regimen. For this error too carries some danger, though much less than the other; yet this error is far more ridiculous than the other.

εἰ 611 γὰρ ἄλλος ἰητρὸς ἢ καὶ ἰδιώτης ἐσελθὼν καὶ γνοὺς τὰ ξυμβεβηκότα δώῃ καὶ φαγεῖν καὶ πιεῖν ἃ ὁ ἕτερος ἐκώλυσεν, ἐπιδήλως ἂν δοκέῃ ὠφεληκέναι. τὰ δὲ τοιάδε μάλιστα καθυβρίζεται τῶν χειρωνακτέων ὑπὸ τῶν ἀνθρώπων, δοκέει γὰρ αὐτέοισιν ὁ ἐσελθὼν ἰητρὸς ἢ ἰδιώτης ὥσπερ εἰ καὶ τεθνειότα ἀναστήσας. Διὰ τὰς ὀδύνας πολλάκις οἱ κάμνοντες ἄσφυκτοι γίγνονται καὶ λειποψυχοῦσι καὶ κίνησιν οὐδεμίαν οἷόν τε κινηθῆναι, κατὰ ταῦτα δὲ καὶ διὰ τὴν ὀξύτητα τῆς νόσου· τινὲς δὲ οὐ διὰ ταῦτα κάμνουσι τὴν δύναμιν, ἀλλὰ διὰ κένωσιν ἀγγείων ὁπωσοῦν γεγονυῖαν, εἴτ᾿ οὖν δι᾿ ἔκκρισιν πρόδηλον αἰσθήσει, εἴτε διαφόρησιν, εἴτε καὶ διὰ λιμαγχονίαν. οὗτοι μὲν οὖν δέονται τροφὴν προσάρασθαι ὅτι τάχιστα· τοὐναντίον δὲ ἂν οἱ δι᾿ ὀδύνην ἢ δι᾿ ὀξύτητα τῆς νόσου, κενώσεως ἐνίοτε χρῄζουσι μᾶλλον ἢ πληρώσεως.

For if 611 another physician, or even a layman, coming in and recognising what has happened, should give him both to eat and to drink the things which the other forbade, he would manifestly seem to have helped. And such matters, among the works of handicraft (τῶν χειρωνακτέων), are especially made objects of contempt by people, for the physician or layman who came in seems to them as if he had even raised up a dead man. Because of pains the patients often become pulseless and faint and are unable to make any movement whatever, and in the same way also because of the acuteness of the disease; but some suffer in their power not on account of these things but on account of an emptying of the vessels that has come about in whatever way, whether through an excretion evident to perception, or through dispersal, or even through starvation. These, then, need to take nourishment as quickly as possible; on the contrary, those who are weak through pain or through the acuteness of the disease sometimes require evacuation rather than repletion.

On account of pains, patients often become pulseless and fall into fainting, and are unable to make any movement at all; and in the same way also on account of the acuteness of the disease. But to these the one who gives nourishment does some very great harm; whereas if one presses those who are weak through emptiness of the vessels with a thin diet, 612 he would harm them, but not in a similarly conspicuous or acute way; yet it is more disgraceful to fail to recognise those who are weak through evacuation than those who are weak through pain. For indeed, if the whole body rests much contrary to its habit, it is not immediately stronger; and if, having been idle for a longer time, it should suddenly come to exertions, it would manifestly fare somewhat badly. And so too each single part of the body.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

613 For indeed the feet would suffer something of this sort, and so would the other joints that are not accustomed to exertion, if after an interval of time they suddenly come to exertion. The eyes too and the teeth and any part whatsoever would suffer these things, since a bed that is soft contrary to custom produces distress, and so does one that is hard contrary to custom, and a couch in the open air contrary to custom hardens the body. He has returned again to the subject set before him, having completed the discussion that fell in his way by digression concerning weakness in respect of the faculty; and he says that what is now to be said is somewhat similar to what was said earlier about the belly, for it tends toward the same main point, in which he establishes that wholesale changes bring great harm to the sick. What he now writes is as follows: just as the belly, having been entirely idle for a rather long time, is not able to perform its function well until it has again gradually been accustomed and brought back to its proper activities, so too the whole body and each of the other parts.

εἰ γάρ τις ἕλκος λαβὼν ἐν κνήμῃ μήτε λίην ἐπίκαιρον μήτε λίην εὔηθες μήτε ἄγαν δυσελκὴς ὢν μήτε ἄγαν εὐελκὴς αὐτίκα ἀρξάμενος ἐκ πρώτης κατακείμενος ἰητρεύοιτο καὶ μηδαμῇ μετεωρίζοι τὸ σκέλος, ἀφλέγμαντον μὲν ἂν οὕτως εἴη καὶ ὑγιὴς πολλῷ θᾶσσον οὕτω γένοιτο ἢ εἰ περιπλανώμενος ἰητρεύοιτο· εἰ μέντοι πεμπταῖος ἢ ἑκταῖος ἢ καὶ ἔτι ἀνωτέρω ἀναστὰς ἐθέλει προβαίνειν, μᾶλλον ἂν πονέοι τότε ἢ εἰ αὐτίκα ἔξ ἀρχῆς πλανώμενος ἰητρεύοιτο· εἰ δὲ καὶ πολλὰ ταλαιπωρήσειεν 615 ἐξαπίνης, πολλῷ ἂν μᾶλλον πονέσειεν ἢ εἰ ἐκεῖνος ἰητρευόμενος ταῦτα ταλαιπωρήσειεν ἐν ταύτῃσι τῇσιν ἡμέρῃσιν. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἔξ ἅπαντος, ἀνωτέρω

For if someone who has received a wound in the shin, neither too critical nor too benign, and who is himself neither excessively bad at healing wounds nor excessively good at healing them, were to be treated lying down, beginning at once from the first day, and were in no way to raise the leg, he would in this way be free from inflammation and would in this way become healthy much faster than if he were treated while walking about; if, however, on the fifth or on the sixth day, or even still earlier, he gets up and wishes to walk forward, he would suffer more at that point than if he had been treated walking about at once from the beginning; and if he should also suddenly exert himself a great deal, 615 he would suffer much more than if the former man, being treated in that way, should exert himself in these same things on these days. He is still making his argument about the sudden change: just as before in the case of other examples, so now too in the case of a wound. For if someone, he says, having a wound in the shin, when he ought to keep quiet and lie down, were to go about walking gently (for this is what 'treated while walking about' signifies), he would indeed be harmed in any case, but less than the man who kept quiet during the first days and then suddenly, around the 5th or 6th day, or even earlier than that, around the 3rd or 4th, began to walk. Some people, however, think that 'still earlier' (ἀνωτέρω) is said of the days that follow, the 7th and 8th and 9th; but these people ought, if this was what pleased them, to write the beginning of the word with the letters epsilon and xi, ἐξωτέρω, and not ἀνωτέρω with alpha and nu. 'What then,' they will say, 'could there not sometimes be a wound of such a kind that even if someone begins to walk on the seventh or eighth day, having kept quiet beforehand, he is harmed in no small degree?'

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπίκαιρον, ἀνωτέρω, ἔτι ἀνωτέρω τοῦ περὶ γ΄ ἢ δ΄, ἢ εἰ ἐκεῖνος ἰητρευόμενος ταῦτα ταλαιπωρήσειεν

For if someone who has received a wound on the shin, neither very serious nor very simple, and who is himself neither very slow to heal nor very quick to heal, were treated lying down right from the first day, and never raised the leg, he would in this way remain free of inflammation and would in this way become healthy much sooner than if he were treated while wandering about. If, however, on the fifth or sixth day, or even further up, he were to get up and wish to walk about, he would then suffer more than if he had been treated while wandering about right from the beginning; and if he were also suddenly to undergo much 615 exertion, he would suffer much more than if that other man, while being treated, had undergone these exertions on these days. He is still discoursing about the sudden change, just as earlier with other examples, so now also with a wound. For if someone, he says, who has a wound on the shin, when he ought to keep quiet and lie down, were to go about walking gently (for this is what 'were treated while wandering about' signifies), he would be harmed in any case, but less than the one who kept quiet on the first days and then suddenly began to walk around the fifth or sixth day, or still further up, around the third or fourth. Some people, however, think that 'further up' has been said of the days that follow, the seventh, eighth and ninth; if this was their view, they ought to have written the beginning of the word with the letters epsilon and xi, 'exōterō' ('further out'), and not 'anōterō' ('further up'), with alpha and nu. 'What then?' they will say. 'Could there not sometimes be such a wound that, even if someone begins to walk on the seventh or eighth day, having kept quiet before, he is harmed not slightly?'

καὶ μεγάλως φαίην ἂν, εἴ γε μέγα τὸ ἕλκος 616 ᾖ ἢ ὁ κάμνων εἴη δυσελκὴς, μᾶλλον δ᾿ εἰ ἄμφω συνέλθοι· εἰ δὲ μήτε μέγα τὸ ἕλκος μήτε ὁ κάμνων δυσελκὴς εἴη, ἰητρεύοιτο δὲ προσηκόντως, καὶ γὰρ καὶ τοῦτο χρὴ προσυπακούεσθαι τῷ λόγῳ, περὶ τὴν ἑβδόμην ἢ ὀγδόην ἡμέραν ἤδη καλῶς ἔχοι καὶ βαδίζειν ἀβλαβῶς δύναται. διὰ τοῦτ᾿ οὖν προσέθηκε τῷ λόγῳ, περὶ μὲν τοῦ ἕλκους αὐτοῦ, μήτε λίην ἐπίκαιρον μήτε λίην εὔηθες, τὸ ἁπλοῦν δηλονότι καλέσας εὔηθες· περὶ δὲ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου, μήτε ἄγαν δυσελκής· ὁ γὰρ τοιοῦτος εἰ μηδὲν ἄλλο περὶ τὴν θεραπείαν ἁμαρτάνοι, μόνον διὰ τὸ βαδίζειν τὰ μέτρια, δέον κατακεῖσθαι, βλαβείη μὲν ἂν, οὐ μὴν οὕτως γε μεγάλως, ὡς εἰ ταῖς πρώταις ἡμέραις ἡσυχάσας, περὶ τρίτην ἢ τετάρτην ἢ πέμπτην ἢ ἕκτην ἤρξατο περιπατεῖν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπίκαιρον, εὔηθες, δυσελκής

And greatly, I would say, if the ulcer 616 be large or the patient be hard to heal of ulcers (δυσελκής), and all the more if both should coincide; but if neither the ulcer be large nor the patient hard to heal, and he be treated in the proper manner—for this too must be supplied in understanding the statement—he would already be in good condition around the seventh or eighth day and is able to walk without harm. For this reason, then, he added to the statement, concerning the ulcer itself, 'neither too critical (ἐπίκαιρον) nor too harmless (εὔηθες)', evidently calling the simple ulcer 'harmless'; and concerning the man, 'nor too hard to heal of ulcers'. For such a man, if he commits no other error in the treatment, would be harmed merely through walking in moderation when he ought to lie down, yet not indeed so greatly as if, having kept still in the first days, he began to walk about around the third or fourth or fifth or sixth.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπίκαιρον, εὔηθες, δυσελκής, βαδίζειν τὰ μέτρια

And greatly, I would say, if the wound 616 is large or the patient heals poorly (δυσελκής), and still more if both coincide. But if neither the wound is large nor the patient heals poorly, and he is treated appropriately (for this too must be understood as implied in the statement), then around the seventh or eighth day he would already be in a good state and is able to walk without harm. It is for this reason, then, that he added to the statement, concerning the wound itself, 'neither very serious nor very mild', evidently calling the simple wound 'mild' (εὔηθες); and concerning the person, 'nor exceedingly a poor healer'. For such a person, if he committed no other error in the treatment, but only walked moderately when he ought to have been lying down, would indeed be harmed, yet not so greatly as if, having kept still on the first days, he had begun to walk about on the third or fourth or fifth or sixth day.

Greek (Kühn)

ὁ δὲ μέγα τὸ ἕλκος ἔχων καὶ δυσελκὴς ὢν οὐ μετρίως, ἀλλὰ μεγάλως ἂν βλαβείη περιπατῶν ἐξ ἀρχῆς, ὥσπερ καὶ πάλιν ὁ μικρόν γε τὸ ἕλκος ἔχων καὶ λίαν ὢν εὐελκὴς ἐν ταῖς πρώταις ἡμέραις 617 ἡσυχάσας περὶ τὴν ε΄ ἢ στ΄ ἤρξατο περιπατεῖν, οὐκ ἂν μεγάλως, ἀλλὰ μετρίως βλαβείη. διὰ ταῦτ᾿ οὖν ὁ Ἱπποκράτης ἐπὶ τοῦ μέσως ἔχοντος ἐν ἀμφοτέραις ταῖς ἀντιθέσεσι τὸν λόγον ἐποιήσατο· λέγει δὲ ἀντίθεσιν τήν τε κατὰ τὸ μέγα καὶ μικρὸν ἕλκος καὶ τὴν κατὰ τὸ δυσελκῆ καὶ εὐελκῆ εἶναι τὸν ἄνθρωπον. εὔδηλον δ᾿ ὅτι διαφέρει μηδὲν ἐπίκαιρον ἕλκος εἰπεῖν ἢ μέγα, καθάπερ οὐδὲ μικρὸν ἢ εὔηθες· τὸ γὰρ ἐπίκαιρον ἢ διὰ τὴν τοῦ χωρίου φύσιν γίνεται τοιοῦτον, μέγα κατὰ δύναμιν ἀποτελούμενον, εἰ καὶ τῇ διαιρέσει τοῦ χρωτὸς εἴη μικρὸν ἢ καὶ κατ᾿ αὐτὸ τὸ τῆς διαιρέσεως μέγεθος ἐπίκαιρον γένηται· τὸ δ᾿ εὔηθες, ὅπερ ἀπλοῦν ἐστι καὶ μικρὸν, ἢ διὰ τὴν τοῦ χωρίου φύσιν ἄκυρον οὖσαν, ἢ κατ᾿ αὐτὴν τὴν διαίρεσιν βραχεῖαν γενομένην.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπίκαιρον, χρωτὸς, ἄκυρον, εὔηθες

But the man who has a large wound and is slow to heal would be harmed not moderately but greatly by walking about from the beginning, just as, conversely, the man who has a small wound and is very easy to heal, if, having kept quiet during the first days, 617 he began to walk about around the fifth or sixth day, would be harmed not greatly but moderately. For these reasons, then, Hippocrates framed his statement with reference to the case that stands in the middle in both of the oppositions; and by 'opposition' is meant both that between a large and a small wound and that between the man's being slow to heal and easy to heal. And it is quite clear that it makes no difference whether one says a 'critical' wound or a 'large' one, just as neither does 'small' or 'mild'; for the critical wound becomes such either because of the nature of the place, being rendered large in its effect even if it be small in respect of the division of the flesh, or else it becomes critical in respect of the very magnitude of the division; while the mild one, which is simple and small, is such either because of the nature of the place, which is of no vital importance, or in respect of the division itself, which is slight.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπίκαιρον, εὔηθες, δυσελκὴς, εὐελκὴς, χρωτὸς, κατὰ δύναμιν

But the one who has a large wound and is a poor healer of wounds would be harmed not moderately but greatly by walking about from the beginning; just as, again, the one who has a small wound and is a very good healer, having kept quiet in the first days 617 and begun to walk about around the fifth or sixth, would be harmed not greatly but moderately. For these reasons, then, Hippocrates framed his statement with reference to the one who is in a middle condition in both of the antitheses; and by antithesis he means both the one concerning a large and a small wound and the one concerning the person's being a poor healer or a good healer of wounds. It is quite clear that it makes no difference whether one calls a wound serious or large, just as it makes none whether one calls it small or mild; for the serious wound becomes such either through the nature of the place, being rendered large in its power, even if it be small in the division of the flesh, or it becomes serious by the very magnitude of the division; while the mild wound, which is simple and small, is so either through the nature of the place, which is of no consequence, or through the division itself having been slight.

Greek (Kühn)

Διὰ τέλεος οὖν μαρτυρέει ταῦτα πάντα ἀλλήλοισιν, ὅτι πάντα 618 ἐξαπίνης μείζω πολλῷ τοῦ μετρίου μεταβαλλόμενα καὶ ἐπὶ τὰ καὶ ἐπὶ τὰ βλάπτει. Ἐφ᾿ ἑκάστῳ τῶν παραδειγμάτων ἀναμιμνήσκει τοῦ προκειμένου λόγου. καὶ νῦν οὖν ἐπὶ τοῦ κατὰ τὸ ἕλκος παραδείγματος ἐποίησεν αὐτό. τὸ γὰρ διὰ τέλεος ταὐτὸν σημαίνει τῷ διὰ παντὸς καὶ ἀεί. Πολλαπλασίη μὲν οὖν κατὰ κοιλίην ἡ βλάβη ἐστὶν, ἢν ἐκ πολλῆς κενεαγγείης ἐξαπίνης πλέον τοῦ μετρίου προσαίρηται· ἀτὰρ καὶ κατὰ τὸ ἄλλο σῶμα, ἢν ἐκ πολλῆς ἡσυχίης ἐξαίφνης ἐς πλείω πόνον ἔλθοι, πουλὺ πλείω βλαβείη ἢ εἰ ἐκ πολλῆς ἐδωδῆς ἐς κενεαγγείην μεταβάλλοι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Throughout, then, all these things bear witness to one another, that all things 618 which are changed suddenly by much more than the measure do harm, both in the one direction and in the other. At each of the examples he recalls the proposition under discussion; and so now too he has done this in the case of the example concerning the wound. For "throughout" (dia teleos) signifies the same as "in every case" and "always". The harm with respect to the belly, then, is many times greater if, after much emptying of the vessels (kenangeia), one suddenly takes in more than the measure; but also with respect to the rest of the body, if after much rest one should suddenly come to greater exertion, one would be harmed much more than if one were to change from much eating to emptying of the vessels.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προσαίρηται

Throughout, then, all these things bear witness to one another, that all things 618 which are changed suddenly by much more than the moderate do harm, in the one direction and in the other. At each of the examples he reminds us of the argument under discussion. And so now too he has done this in the case of the example concerning the wound. For 'throughout' (διὰ τέλεος) signifies the same as 'through everything' and 'always'. Many times greater, then, is the harm in respect of the belly if, after much emptiness of the vessels, one suddenly takes in more than the moderate; but also in respect of the rest of the body, if after much rest one should suddenly come to greater exertion, one would be harmed far more than if one should change from much eating to emptiness of the vessels.

Greek (Kühn)

δεῖ μέντοι καὶ τὸ σῶμα τουτέοισιν ἐλινύειν, κἢν ἐκ πολλῆς ταλαιπωρίης ἐξαπίνης ἐς σχολήν τε καὶ ῥαθυμίην ἐκπέσῃ· δεῖ δὲ καὶ τουτέοισι τὴν κοιλίην ἐλινύειν πλήθεος βρώμης· 619 εἰ δὲ μὴ, πόνον ἐν τῷ σώματι ἐμποιήσει καὶ βάρος ὅλου τοῦ σώματος. Ὃ πρῶτον ἁπάντων ἀποδεῖξαι προὔθετο, τοῦτ᾿ ἦν αὐτὸ τὸ περὶ τῶν κατὰ τὴν κοιλίην ἀθρόων μεταβολῶν· εἶτα ἀπ᾿ αὐτοῦ πρὸς τὸ γενικώτερον, ὃ δὴ καὶ καθολικώτερον ὀνομάζουσι, μεταβὰς, περὶ πάσης ἔθους ἐξαλλαγῆς ἀθρόας διελέχθη μέχρι δεῦρο. περιεχομένων γὰρ τῶν εἰδικωτέρων ἐν τοῖς γενικωτέροις καὶ τῶν κατὰ μέρος ἐν τοῖς καθόλου καὶ ἡ τῶν κατὰ τὴν κοιλίην ἐξαλλαγὴ συναποδέδεικται τῇ περὶ πάντων ἀθρόᾳ μεταβολῇ· ἀλλὰ νυνὶ πάλιν ἐπὶ τὴν κοιλίαν ἀφικόμενος, τῶν ἐν αὐτῇ μεταβολῶν τὰς διαφορὰς διδάσκει, καί φησι πολλαπλασίαν εἶναι τὴν βλάβην ἐν τῇ μεταβολῇ τῆς διαίτης, ὅταν ἐκ πολλῆς ἀσιτίας ἀθρόως προσεπενέγκηται πλείω τῶν συμμέτρων, ἤπερ ὅταν ἐκ πολλῆς ἐδωδῆς εἰς ἀσιτίαν μεταβάλλῃ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

It is necessary, however, for the body also to rest in these cases, and if one suddenly falls from much hardship into leisure and idleness, in these cases too the belly must rest from an abundance of food; 619 otherwise it will produce distress in the body and heaviness of the whole body. What he proposed first of all to demonstrate was precisely this matter concerning the all-at-once changes in the belly; then, passing from this to the more generic, which indeed they also call the more universal, he discoursed up to this point about every all-at-once alteration of habit. For since the more specific are contained in the more generic, and the particular in the universal, the alteration in the belly too has been demonstrated along with the all-at-once change in all things; but now, coming back again to the belly, he teaches the differences of the changes in it, and says that the harm is many times greater in the change of regimen when, after much abstinence from food, one all at once takes in more than what is proportionate, than when one changes from much eating to abstinence from food.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κἢν (read as καὶ ἤν, beginning a new conditional clause attached to the second ἐλινύειν; the sentence division of the lemma is uncertain), ἐλινύειν (rest / keep idle; applied here both to the body and to the belly), συμμέτρων (the moderate / commensurate amounts)

"In these cases, however, the body too must rest; and if from much hardship someone suddenly falls into leisure and idleness, in these cases too the belly must rest from a quantity of food; 619 otherwise it will produce pain in the body and heaviness of the whole body." What he set out to demonstrate first of all was precisely this matter of sudden changes concerning the belly. Then, passing from it to the more generic point, which people also call the more universal, he has discussed up to this point every sudden alteration of habit. For since the more specific things are contained in the more generic, and the particulars in the universals, the alteration concerning the belly has also been demonstrated together with the sudden change concerning all things. But now, returning again to the belly, he teaches the differences among the changes that occur in it. He says that the harm in a change of regimen is many times greater when, after much abstinence from food, one suddenly takes more than the moderate amounts, than when one changes from much eating to abstinence from food.

Greek (Kühn)

τούτῳ δ᾿ αὐτῷ πάλιν ὅμοιον παρενέβαλε περὶ τὸ πᾶν σῶμα γιγνόμενον· ἧττον γὰρ βλάπτονται μεταβάλλοντες ἐξαίφνης εἰς ἡσυχίαν ἐκ κινήσεων 620 ἤπερ ὅταν εἰς κίνησιν ἐξ ἡσυχίας. πάλιν δὲ κᾀνταῦθα προσέγραψεν ἐπανορθώματα τῆς ἀκαίρου μεταβολῆς, ὥσπερ κᾀν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν· οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον δὲ οὐδὲ τοῦτο τῷ προκειμένῳ σκέμματι. ἃ δ᾿ οὖν προσέγραψε, ταῦτά ἐστι· τοῦτο μὲν εἰς ἀργίαν μεταβαίνοντας ἐκ κινήσεως τε καὶ πράξεως, ἐλινύειν ἀξιοῖ, τουτέστιν ἡσυχάζειν τῷ παντὶ σώματι· τοῦτο δὲ ταὐτόν ἐστιν ἐκείνῳ τῷ, ὅκου λιμὸς οὐ δεῖ πονέειν· τοὺς δ᾿ εἰς ἡσυχίαν ἐκ πολλῆς ταλαιπωρίας, ὀλιγώτερα προσφέρεσθαι, τοῦτο δ᾿ αὖ πάλιν ἐστὶν ἐλινύειν τὴν κοιλίην· ὡς εἴ γε μὴ πονῶν ὅλῳ τῷ σώματι προσφέροιτο τὰ συνήθη, πλῆθος ὑποθρέψει, διὸ βαρυνθήσεται τὸ πᾶν σῶμα καὶ πονήσειε. σημαίνει δ᾿ αὐτῷ νῦν ὁ πόνος τὴν βλάβην, γενικῶς νοούμενος πολλαῖς ταῖς κατ᾿ εἶδος βλάβαις. Ὁ δὴ οὖν πλεῖστός μοι λόγος γέγονε περὶ τῆς μεταβολῆς τῆς ἐπὶ τῆς διαίτης καὶ ἐπὶ τὰ καὶ ἐπὶ τά.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πολλαῖς ταῖς κατ᾿ εἶδος βλάβαις, πονήσειε

And alongside this very point he again inserted something similar that happens with respect to the whole body: for people are less harmed when they change suddenly to rest from motions 620 than when to motion from rest. Here again, too, he added corrections of the untimely change, just as in the preceding passages; but this too is not necessary for the inquiry before us. What he added, then, is this: on the one hand, those who pass over into idleness from motion and activity he requires to be still, that is, to rest with the whole body; and this is the same as that saying, "where there is hunger one must not toil"; on the other hand, those who pass into rest from much hardship [he requires] to take in less, and this in turn is "to let the belly be still"; since if one who is not toiling with the whole body were to take the customary amounts, he will nourish up a plethora, on account of which the whole body will be weighed down and would suffer pain. And "pain" (ponos) here signifies for him the harm, understood generically with the many harms according to species. "So then my discussion has been at greatest length about the change in regimen, both in the one direction and in the other."

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πονήσειε, γενικῶς νοούμενος πολλαῖς ταῖς κατ᾿ εἶδος βλάβαις, ὑποθρέψει

Again, he inserted alongside this a similar case that occurs with respect to the whole body: people are harmed less when they change suddenly from movements to rest 620 than when they change from rest to movement. And here again he added corrections of the untimely change, just as in the preceding passages; but this too is not necessary for the inquiry at hand. What he added, then, is this. On the one hand, he holds that those who pass from movement and activity to idleness should 'keep still' (ἐλινύειν), that is, be at rest with the whole body; and this is the same as that saying, 'where there is hunger one must not exert oneself'. On the other hand, those who pass to rest from much hardship should take less food, and this in turn is 'the belly keeping still'. For if someone who is not exerting himself with the whole body were to take his customary foods, he will breed a plethora, and so the whole body will be weighed down and would suffer pain. Here 'pain' (πόνος) signifies for him harm, understood generically as covering the many specific kinds of harm. 'Now, I have spoken at greatest length about the change of regimen in the one direction and in the other.'

Συνήγαγε πάλιν αὐτὸς εἰς ἓν κεφάλαιον ἅπαντα τὸν λόγον, ἀξιῶν ἐπὶ μὲν τῶν ὡσαύτως ἐχόντων μὴ μεταβάλλειν τὴν δίαιταν, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν ἐξ ὑγείας εἰς νόσον μεταβάλλειν μὲν, οὐ μὴν ὡς ἄλλοι μεταβάλλουσιν. εἶτα καὶ διὰ συντόμων ἐδήλωσε τὸν σκοπὸν τῆς ἐπὶ τῶν ὀξέως νοσούντων διαίτης.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κενεαγγικὸν, ἐρεθιστικὸν

It is useful, then, 621 to know these things with a view to everything, but also with a view to the matter which the discussion was about, namely that in acute diseases they change over to the gruels from the emptying of the vessels (κενεαγγείη); for one must change as I now direct; and thereafter gruels are not to be used until the disease has become concocted or some other sign appears, either in the intestine a sign of emptying of the vessels (κενεαγγικόν) or of irritation, or in the hypochondria, such signs as will be written. He himself has again gathered the whole discussion into one main point, holding it right, in the case of those who remain in the same condition, not to change the regimen, but in the case of those who pass from health into disease to change it, yet not as others change it. Then he also made clear in brief the aim of the regimen in those who are acutely ill.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κενεαγγικὸν, κελεύω ἤδη

For all purposes, 621 then, it is useful to know these things, and also for the matter the discussion was about, namely that in acute diseases people change over to gruels from emptiness of the vessels. For the change must be made as I now direct; and further, gruels should not be used before the disease has been brought to coction, or some other sign appears, either in the intestine, indicating emptiness of the vessels or irritation, or in the hypochondria, such signs as will be described. He has himself again gathered the whole discussion into one main point, holding that in those who remain in the same condition the regimen should not be changed, whereas in those passing from health into disease it should indeed be changed, though not in the way others change it. Then he also set out, in brief terms, the aim of the regimen for those who are acutely ill.

For if the disease has been concocted, he bade pass over to the administration of the juice; but if it should be unconcocted, not to pass over, unless indeed some symptom destructive of the faculty should be observed, when a rather copious evacuation through the belly or some other 622 preceding irritation supervenes. And under the common name of irritation he included all the things that in whatever manner lead the faculty into weakness, among which are also the biting caused by pungent humours, occurring either in the intestine or in the stomach, and especially at its mouth, and sleeplessness and anger and grief and certain worms that have ascended from the intestines to the stomach, and itching of the whole skin or even of certain parts, setting in during the night, so as to break down the faculty not only by the irritation itself but also together with sleeplessness. And the hypochondria too, he says, of the sort that will be described, will themselves also jointly indicate the giving of nourishment, or the not giving; for both can be understood, though rather the latter, so far as concerns the sequence of the words.

For if the disease has been ripened, he bade one pass over to the administration of the juice; but if it should be unconcocted, not to pass over, unless perhaps some symptom destructive of the strength should be seen, with a more copious evacuation through the belly or some other 622 preceding irritation supervening. For it is possible to understand both, but rather the one, so far as the sequence of the argument goes.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εἴ γε μὴν

For in bidding one abstain from ptisan before the disease has been concocted—when, that is, it is evidently possible for the patient to hold out—he added: 'unless some sign appear, either one of emptying of the vessels in the bowel, or one of irritation, or in the hypochondria such as will be described'; on the grounds that these sometimes urge one toward the giving of the juice before the 623 disease has been concocted. Perhaps he means hypochondria that are violently dried out and strongly parched. For such things also go together with the corpse-like face described in the Prognostic, of which he says: 'nose sharp, eyes hollow, temples fallen in.' For this concurrence of signs has a hypochondrium drawn in to a considerable degree, so as to deceive one of those not practised into taking it as drawn up; but it is not the same thing to be strongly drawn in on account of dryness and to be drawn up on account of inflammation, even if, to be sure, an excessive dryness of the body does produce a greater tension in the diaphragm and in the hypochondrium. One must especially pay attention and distinguish inflammation from dryness, since the treatment too is different: for those who have this very condition on account of inflammation there is need of the thinnest regimen, whereas for those who have it on account of dryness there is need of the opposite, in which we give a little juice of ptisan more frequently.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἑφθότητα, τύποις, μετώπου

624 Strong sleeplessness makes drinks and foods raw and more unconcocted; and the change in turn to the opposite loosens the body and produces a boiled condition (ἑφθότης) and heaviness of the head. I have often considered that this book was found in draft form and published after the death of Hippocrates. For the disorder of the arguments appears to have come about in no other way than this: the examples that he had found were written in addition into the book by Hippocrates himself, for the confirmation of the universal statement, some on the front of the sheet and some on the back, while the person who copied them in was not able to assign to each its proper place.

Greek (Kühn)

τὸ γοῦν ἐν τῇ προκειμένῃ ῥήσει λεγόμενον ὑπ᾿ αὐτοῦ φανερωτάτην ἔχει ἀταξίαν· οὐ γὰρ νῦν, ἀλλ᾿ ὀλίγον ἔμπροσθεν ἦν ὁ καιρὸς αὐτοῦ, κατ᾿ ἐκεῖνον τὸν λόγον, ἐν ᾧ περὶ τῆς παρὰ τὸ ἔθος εἰς τοὐναντίον μεταβολῆς ἔγραψε παραδείγματα· προσγράψαι γὰρ ἐκείνοις 625 ἐχρῆν καὶ τοῦτο, τῆς μὲν εἰς ἀγρυπνίαν ἀήθους μεταβολῆς ἀπεπτότερα ποιούσης τὰ προσφερόμενα σιτία τε καὶ ποτὰ, τῆς δ᾿ εἰς ὕπνον πολὺν ἀήθως καὶ τοῦτον ἐπιτηδευθέντα νωθρότητα τοῦ σώματος καὶ καρηβαρίαν. ἣν γὰρ εἶπεν ἑφθότητά τε καὶ διάλυσιν καὶ καρηβαρίαν, ἑνὶ ῥήματι περιληφθεῖσαν τὴν ἔκλυσιν δηλοῖ τοῦ τόνου· συνενδείκνυται δὲ καὶ τὸ τῆς ἑφθότητος ὄνομα, δι᾿ ὑγρότητα θερμὴν γιγνομένης, οὐ διὰ ξηρότητα· πεφυκυίας διαφορεῖν μὲν καὶ ξηραίνειν τῆς ἐγρηγόρσεως, μὴ πέπτειν δὲ κατὰ τὸ βάθος ὑγραίνειν δὲ τό τε πνεῦμα καὶ τὰς ἀποῤῥοίας κατέχειν τοῦ ὕπνου. κατὰ λόγον οὖν τὰ ἀτμώδη περιττώματα πλεονάζει ἐν τῷ σώματι τῶν ἀήθως ἐπὶ πολὺ κοιμηθέντων καὶ διὰ ταῦτα τὴν ὅλην αὐτῶν κατάστασιν ἔκλυτον ὁμοίως γίγνεσθαι τοῖς ἑψημένοις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μὴ πέπτειν δὲ κατὰ τὸ βάθος, ἑφθότητος

At any rate, what is said by him in the passage before us displays a most evident disorder; for its proper occasion was not now, but a little earlier, in that discussion in which he wrote examples concerning the change contrary to custom into the opposite; for this too ought to have been added to those 625, since the unaccustomed change to sleeplessness makes the foods and drinks that are taken more unconcocted, while the change to much sleep, this too practised in an unaccustomed way, produces sluggishness of the body and heaviness of the head. For what he called boiledness and dissolution and heaviness of the head he indicates, comprehended in a single expression, as the relaxation of tone; and the term 'boiledness' also indicates in addition that this comes about through hot moisture, not through dryness; since wakefulness is of such a nature as to disperse and to dry, whereas it belongs to sleep not to concoct in the depth but to moisten the pneuma and to hold back the effluences. Reasonably, then, the vaporous residues abound in the body of those who have slept long contrary to custom, and on account of these their whole condition becomes slack, similarly to things that have been boiled.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἣν γὰρ εἶπεν ἑφθότητά τε καὶ διάλυσιν καὶ καρηβαρίαν, ἑνὶ ῥήματι περιληφθεῖσαν, δι᾿ ὑγρότητα θερμὴν γιγνομένης, μὴ πέπτειν δὲ κατὰ τὸ βάθος, διαφορεῖν, γίγνεσθαι

At any rate, what he says in the present passage shows a most evident disorder; for its proper place was not here but a little earlier, in that discussion in which he wrote examples concerning the change, contrary to habit, into the opposite. For this too ought to have been added to those 625: that the unaccustomed change to sleeplessness makes the foods and drinks that are taken less digestible, while the change to much sleep, unaccustomedly and deliberately pursued at that, makes sluggishness of the body and heaviness of the head. For what he called 'boiledness' and 'dissolution' and 'heaviness of the head', comprehended in a single word, signifies the slackening of the tone. The name 'boiledness' also indicates in addition that this slackening comes about through hot moisture, not through dryness. For wakefulness is by nature such as to disperse and to dry, but not to concoct in the depth, whereas sleep is by nature such as to moisten the pneuma and to retain the effluxes. It is reasonable, then, that the vaporous residues abound in the body of those who have slept for a long time contrary to their habit, and that for this reason their whole condition becomes slack, like things that have been boiled.

626 HIPPOCRATES' BOOK ON REGIMEN IN ACUTE DISEASES AND GALEN'S COMMENTARY III. Sweet wine and vinous wine, both white and dark, and melikraton and water and oxymel: in acute diseases one must distinguish these by the following indications. Not only in a disorderly way, but also incompletely, has he drawn up the catalogue of the wines.
